OK, now I understand the champion. The logic is:
- s0 (GoToColor blue): move toward blue → if on black floor go to GoAwayColor(green) OR if see green go explore
- s1 (Stop): wait → if gray floor go back to GoToColor(blue), if see green flee
- s2 (GoAwayColor green): flee green → if see blue go toward it, if on black stop
- s3 (Explore): wander → if see blue go to it, if on gray flee green

This is quite clever! The robots navigate toward the blue strip and stay there.

**Why D=1 from s0 goes to s2 (GoAwayColor green) when on black floor?** Maybe when on black floor approaching blue, the robot wants to consolidate by fleeing green rather than continuing to approach blue. Or it could be that black floor could also mean the green-side strip, so fleeing green is correct.

Now let me generate 14 machines:

**Machine 1: Champion (exact)**

**Machines 2-7: Small variations of champion**

**Machines 8-14: Exploration**

For exploration, I want to try:
- Adding a 3rd transition to state 1 (Stop) that goes to GoToColor when seeing blue
- Swapping the s0→s2 transition to s0→s1 (stop directly on black floor)
- Trying different floor conditions
- 3-state simplified version of champion
- Adding neighbor-based transitions


So:
- s0 (GoToColor blue): if on black floor → explore (s2), if random → explore (s2)
- s1 (Stop): if gray floor → GoToColor (s0), if see green → explore (s2)
- s2 (Explore): if see blue → GoToColor (s0), if gray floor → stop (s1)?

Wait n2x1: D=1 from s2 = s1 (Stop). C=1 gray floor p=0.4. So if exploring and on gray, stop? That's odd. But it scored 4971.

Actually, maybe the logic is: s0→s2 on black means "if I'm on black floor while going to blue, I might be in the right place but let me explore a bit (random walk to consolidate)". Then s2 (explore) → s0 if sees blue (find blue wall again), s2 → s1 if gray (wait, if on gray while exploring, stop? That doesn't help).

Hmm, maybe n2x1 should be s2→s0 when gray? But D=1=s1. It's a weird machine but it works.

Let me not overthink and just generate good candidates. Let me try a cleaner version where:
- s0 = GoToColor(blue): go to blue wall
- s1 = Stop: stay when on black floor  
- s2 = GoAwayColor(green): flee green walls
- s3 = Explore: random walk when lost

With cleaner transitions:
- s0: if black floor → s1 (stop), if see green → s2 (flee), random small prob → s3
- s1: if gray floor → s0 (go back), if see green → s2 (flee)
- s2: if see blue → s0, if black floor → s1 (stop)
- s3: if see blue → s0, if gray → s2

This is essentially the champion. Good.

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 3:**

There's a massive gap between rank 6 (5266) and rank 7 (6411), and an even bigger gap between rank 8 (7115) and rank 9 (19328). The top 6 machines all share the same core architecture:
- s0 = GoToColor(blue=3): navigate toward blue walls
- s1 = Stop: stay put when in target region
- s2 = GoAwayColor(green=2): flee green walls  
- s3 = Explore(rwm=20): random walk

The champion added a 4th transition to s0: seeing blue color → GoToColor (self-reinforcing). This was the key improvement from round 2 to round 3 (4756→3671).

**What works (top 6 share):**
- GoToColor(3=blue) as primary state
- Stop when on black floor (p≈0.85-0.95)
- Flee green when seen (p≈0.75-0.80)
- GoAwayColor(green=2) → see blue (p≈0.65-0.75) → GoToColor
- Explore → see blue → GoToColor, gray floor → GoAwayColor

**Champion's key feature:** 4 transitions in s0, including seeing blue → GoToColor (D=0=s1? No wait...). 

Let me re-read champion's s0 transitions:
- n0x0: D=1→s2(GoAwayColor), C=0 black floor p=0.9
- n0x1: D=2→s3(Explore), C=7 sees green p=0.8
- n0x2: D=2→s3(Explore), C=5 random p=0.05
- n0x3: D=0→s1(Stop), C=7 sees blue p=0.7

So when in GoToColor(blue) state:
- Black floor → GoAwayColor(green) [consolidate in strip by fleeing green]
- See green → Explore
- Random → Explore  
- See blue → Stop [already near blue wall, stop]

And s1 (Stop) has 3 transitions:
- n1x0: D=0→s0(GoToColor), C=1 gray floor p=0.6
- n1x1: D=2→s3(Explore)... wait D=1 from s1 = s2(GoAwayColor), D=2=s3. n1x1: D=2→s3, C=7 sees green p=0.7... 

Wait, let me recheck. From s1, others=[0,2,3]: D=0=s0, D=1=s2, D=2=s3.
- n1x0: D=0→s0(GoToColor), C=1 gray p=0.6
- n1x1: D=2→s3(Explore)? No, D=2=s3. Condition sees green (l=2), p=0.7
- n1x2: D=0→s0(GoToColor), C=7 sees blue p=0.5

So from Stop: gray→GoToColor, see green→Explore(?), see blue→GoToColor. That means if stopped and see green you explore, and if stopped and see blue you also go toward blue. Makes sense - if see green you're near wrong strip so explore; if see blue you want to consolidate.

**Exploitation strategy:**
Focus on tuning the champion's parameters:

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 4:**

The top 8 machines all score below 4200, and the gap between rank 8 (4140) and rank 9 (18817) is enormous. All top 8 share the same core:
- s0 = GoToColor(blue=3)
- s1 = Stop
- s2 = GoAwayColor(green=2)  
- s3 = Explore(rwm≈15-20)

The champion (3246) differs from the previous champion (3671) in one key way: **n0x0 D=0→s1(Stop) instead of D=1→s2(GoAwayColor)** when on black floor. So when approaching blue and hitting black floor, go directly to Stop (not GoAwayColor). This makes more sense: black floor = you're in a strip, stop there.

The champion's structure:

**Champion parameters (2994.67):**
- p0x0=0.9 (black→Stop), p0x1=0.8 (green→Explore), p0x2=0.03 (random→Explore), p0x3=0.75 (blue→Stop)
- p1x0=0.62 (gray→GoToColor), p1x1=0.72 (green→Explore), p1x2=0.52 (blue→GoToColor)
- p2x0=0.72 (blue→GoToColor), p2x1=0.52 (black→Stop)
- p3x0=0.62 (blue→GoToColor), p3x1=0.38 (gray→GoAwayGreen)

The champion differs from the previous champion mainly in slightly adjusted probabilities. The trend shows probabilities around 0.62-0.72 for color detections and 0.38-0.52 for floor conditions work well.

**What structural variations scored well:**
- Rank 6 (3137): s1 with 4 transitions including random→GoToColor (p=0.02)
- Rank 7 (3156): s3 with 3 transitions adding black→Stop (D=1=s1, p=0.75)
- Rank 8 (3171): s1 with 4 transitions including black→GoToColor (p=0.8)
- Rank 9 (3199): s2 with 3 transitions adding gray→Explore (p=0.3)
- Rank 10 (3248): s2 with gray→Explore AND s3 with black→Stop

**Strategy for Round 6:**
1. **Elitism:** Champion exact copy
2. **Fine exploitation:** Try champion with small probability nudges
3. **Structural exploitation:** Combine best structural variants with champion parameters
4. **Exploration:** Try lower p0x2 (random escape) values, different rwm3, try adding black→Stop to s3 with champion probabilities, try adding more transitions

Key parameters to explore:
- p0x2: champion=0.03, try 0.02, 0.04, 0.05
- p0x3: champion=0.75, try 0.72, 0.78, 0.80
- p1x1: champion=0.72, try 0.68, 0.75, 0.78
- p2x0: champion=0.72, try 0.68, 0.75, 0.78
- p3x1: champion=0.38, try 0.35, 0.40, 0.42
- rwm3: champion=20, try 15, 25

## Reasoning

**Key observations from Round 8:**

The champion (2938.12) from round 6 still hasn't been beaten. All round 8 machines score 3056-3176 (except the random one at 22728). The champion is the global best.

**What I observe:**
- The best machines in round 8 combine: s1 with 4 transitions (adding random→GoToColor p=0.02) AND s3 with 3 transitions (adding black→Stop)
- Rank 1 (3056): s1 has 4 trans + s3 has 3 trans (black→Stop p=0.78)
- Rank 2 (3085): same but s3 black→Stop p=0.80
- Rank 3 (3102): s1 has 4 trans, s3 has 2 trans (the round 7 rank-1 machine repeated)

The champion itself has s1 with 3 transitions and s3 with 2 transitions. Adding the 4th to s1 and 3rd to s3 seems to help but not enough to beat the champion. This might be due to stochastic noise in the simulation.

**Strategy for Round 9 (only 2 rounds left):**
1. Champion exact copy (elitism)
2. Best structural variant: s1 4-trans + s3 3-trans with champion params (try different p values for the extra transitions)
3. Try combining all three structural extensions with champion params
4. Try slightly different base params for the champion core
5. Try p1x3 (random from Stop) = 0.03, 0.015, 0.025
6. Try s3 black→Stop with different probs: 0.70, 0.75, 0.80, 0.85
7. One genuine exploration attempt

I need to be very careful to include `--n<I>`, `--cle<I>`, and all destination indices. Let me write each machine carefully.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

At every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is outside the target region. The score does not depend on how close the robots are to each other.

The arena has two strips at opposite ends. The floor is BLACK in both strips and GRAY in between. The target region is the strip whose bordering walls are BLUE; the walls bordering the other strip are GREEN.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 20 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.50 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=6  Spiral        -> traces an expanding spiral
            requires --spd<I>  integer in [1..100]  (spiral speed)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=10  FlockCenter   -> moves toward the centre of the group
            requires --fc<I>  integer in [1..100]  (flocking gain)
  B=11  ZigZag        -> moves in a zigzag pattern
            requires --zfr<I>  integer in [1..100]  (zigzag frequency)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
